$\Phi_k(c):=\max_{P_k}[\langle c,\xi\rangle-D(\xi)]\ge h_{P_{k+1}}(c)$.
The vectors $\theta_y$ are $0$, $\epsilon_i$, $\alpha\epsilon_i$ with
$\alpha\in\{1-2^{-r}:1\le r\le e\}$, and
$\tfrac12(\epsilon_i+\lambda\epsilon_o)$, so every such $c$ has
$c_1\ge0\ge c_2$ and lies in
\[
  \mathcal C=\{(\beta,0)\}\cup\{(1,-\alpha)\}\cup
  \bigl\{(1-\tfrac\lambda2,-\tfrac12),(\tfrac12,-\tfrac\lambda2)\bigr\}.
\]
Evaluating at $A_0$, $A_4$ and $A_2$, where $D(A_0)=0$,
$D(A_4)=\min(\cdot,\gamma+\tfrac12(M_{k+1}-\lambda M_k))=0$ and
$D(A_2)\le2^{-e}M_k$, gives
$\Phi_k(c)\ge\max\bigl(0,\ c_1M_{k+1}+c_2M_k,\ c_1M_k+c_2M_{k+1}-2^{-e}M_k\bigr)$.
If $c_2=0$ then $h_{P_{k+1}}(c)=\beta M_{k+1}$, attained at $A_4$. If
$c_2<0$, two bounds are needed. For
$\Phi_k(c)\ge c_1M_{k+1}+c_2M_{k+2}$, the point $A_2$ and
$M_{k+1}-M_{k+2}=\lambda\delta_k$ give a difference
$(c_1+\lambda c_2)\delta_k-2^{-e}M_k$, and $\delta_k\ge uM_k$ reduces it to
$u(c_1+\lambda c_2)\ge2^{-e}$; now $c_1+\lambda c_2$ is $1-\lambda\alpha\ge u$
at $(1,-\alpha)$, is $u$ at $(1-\tfrac\lambda2,-\tfrac12)$ and is
$\tfrac12u(1+\lambda)\ge\tfrac u2$ at $(\tfrac12,-\tfrac\lambda2)$, so
$u(c_1+\lambda c_2)\ge u^2/2=2^{1-2j}\ge2^{-e}$ by $e\ge2j-1$. For
$\Phi_k(c)\ge c_1\mu$, again at $A_2$ and using $\mu-2\gamma=u\mu$: at
$(1,-\alpha)$ with $\alpha\le1$ the difference is at least
$(u-2^{-e})M_k-u\mu\ge0$ whenever $M_k\ge\mu/(1-2^{\,j-1-e})$, which
follows from $M_k\ge\mu^{\dagger}$; at $(1-\tfrac\lambda2,-\tfrac12)$ the
same expression appears; and at $(\tfrac12,-\tfrac\lambda2)$ the difference
is $(\tfrac12u(1+\lambda)-2^{-e})M_k-\tfrac12u(1+\lambda)\mu$, nonnegative
once $M_k\ge\mu/(1-2^{1-e}/(u(1+\lambda)))$, and
$2^{1-e}/(u(1+\lambda))\le2^{\,j-e}$.

The $\Vmax\times\Vmax$ composites reduce to the same estimate through
$\max_i\min_j\le\min_j\max_i$ and the branch $a_{1,1}$, and need only
$e\ge2j-2$. Silence is then \Cref{cor:wedge-cert}: on $P_k$ one has
$\Lambda(P_k)(v_1,a_{1,1})=2^{-e}M_k>0$ and
$\Lambda(P_k)(v_1,b_1)\ge\gamma+\mu/2>0$, while
$\Lambda(P_k)(a_{1,1},v_1)=0$ at $A_0$ and $\Lambda(P_k)(b_1,v_1)=0$ at
$A_4$, so no entry the test reads is decisive. The transfer to
\Cref{def:slack}, \Cref{def:trans-slack} and \Cref{def:transport} is that
each is dominated by the hybrid entrywise.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The count]\label{cor:wedge-count}
For $e=2j$ and $m=j+4$, so $N=6j+9$, one has
$M_k=\lambda^k\tfrac9{16}-\tfrac1{16}$ and
\[
  K=1,\,6,\,15,\,33,\,68,\,138,\,279,\,560\qquad(j=2,\dots,9),
\]
with $K\ge2^{\,j-1}$ for $j\ge3$ and hence $K\ge2^{(N-15)/6}$. The
silence of \Cref{thm:wedge-proved} is therefore $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds,
unconditionally.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The closed form is the linear recursion with
$2\gamma/(1-\lambda)=2^{\,j-m}=\tfrac1{16}$. For $j\ge3$ put
$n:=2^{\,j-1}\ge4$; since $(1-1/n)^n$ increases in $n$,
$\lambda^n\ge(3/4)^4>0.316$ and $M_n>0.316\cdot\tfrac9{16}-\tfrac1{16}>0.115$,
while $\mu^{\dagger}=2^{-j-3}/(\lambda(1-2^{-j}))$ decreases in $j$ from
$\tfrac1{42}<0.024$ at $j=3$; so $M_{2^{j-1}}>\mu^{\dagger}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:wedge}
The mechanism is the wedge. In the coordinates $\xi=z|_C-\val|_C$ the polytope
$Q(WD)-\val$ is
$\{\xi\in[0,\tfrac12]^2:\lambda\xi_1-2\gamma\le\xi_2\le(\xi_1+2\gamma)/\lambda\}$
with $\lambda=1-2^{1-j}$, a thin wedge with apex at the origin; the two
hyperplanes $\{g_{v_i,\bar i}=0\}$ support it at the apex and touch it there
without containing it, which is exactly \Cref{cor:wedge-cert}, and each round
shrinks the wedge by the factor $\lambda$ only. The chain $P_k$ of \Cref{def:wedge-chain} makes this
unconditional: \Cref{thm:wedge-proved} proves the clause condition of
\Cref{thm:hybrid-convex-barrier} for every $j\ge2$, $e\ge2j-1$,
$m\ge j+2$, and \Cref{cor:wedge-count} turns it into $2^{\Omega(N)}$
silent rounds. \Cref{prop:wedge} remains the sharper statement at the
sizes it covers --- the certificate is two rounds short of the measurement
throughout --- but it is no longer the only one. We verified the proof's
load-bearing inequality, $\Phi_k(c)\ge h_{P_{k+1}}(c)$ at every direction
and every $k\le K$, in exact rational arithmetic over the $60$ triples
$j\le6$, $e\in\{2j-1,2j,2j+1,3j\}$, $m\in\{j+2,j+4,j+6\}$, and the
conclusion $\Delta_k\ge\Lambda(P_k)$ directly against a run of the hybrid
at $j=2,3,4$, with no violation in either.

The scope is narrower than it first appears, and this is the important
qualification. $WD$ is one-player, so by \Cref{thm:seed-dichotomy} the seeded
calculus \Cref{def:seeded} decides both Max vertices at slack round zero. It
does not defeat \Cref{def:simorder} either, and for a different reason: the
greatest fixed point there contains the pair $(v_i,a_{i,1})$, so
$w^\ast(v_i)\le w^\ast(a_{i,1})$ is derived, which at a Max vertex forces
equality and proves $a_{i,1}$ optimal --- firing direction (i) of
\Cref{rem:own-successor}, at round zero. We verified this on $WD(4,2,6)$ and
$WD(6,3,7)$, the preorder being sound there with no violation among its derived
pairs. So the family defeats \Cref{def:slack}, \Cref{def:trans-slack},
\Cref{def:transport} and the hybrid of \Cref{sec:hybrid}, and neither
\Cref{def:simorder} nor \Cref{sec:seeded}. It defeats the multiplicative mechanisms as well: on
$WD(2j,j,j+4)$ the min-times closure of \Cref{def:ratio} first fires at
rounds $30$ and $87$ for $j=2,3$ and not within $170$ rounds for $j=4$
--- $N=21,27,33$ --- against $21$, $51$ and $112$ for
\Cref{def:trans-slack}, so it is defeated by the same family and is on
this one the slower of the two, having been the faster on $H_m$
(\Cref{prop:ratio-closure}). Among the mechanisms that \emph{propagate}
a matrix, the wedge is therefore the one obstruction here that none
evades; the two mechanisms it fails against, \Cref{def:simorder} and
\Cref{sec:seeded}, both decide it without iterating at all.

One further attempt is worth recording because it looks like the right
one and is not. In the coordinates $\xi$ a multiplicative cut
$z(p)\le R(p,q)z(q)$ reads
$\xi(p)\le R\xi(q)+(R\,w^\ast(q)-w^\ast(p))$, whose constant vanishes
when $R$ is exact; an exact ratio cut is therefore a \emph{cone through
the apex}, which is the shape the wedge is made of. Adjoining all such
cuts, with $R$ taken from \Cref{def:ratio} and its min-times closure, to
the difference cuts of \Cref{def:hybrid} changes nothing: on
$WD(2j,j,j+4)$ for $j=2,3,4$ the first firing round remains $4,8,17$,
and the polygon is \emph{identical} at every round --- on $WD(4,2,6)$
the augmented programme adds $346$ to $348$ ratio cuts per round beside
the $218$ to $372$ difference cuts and the vertex set never moves. The
reason is that a ratio read off the polytope is by construction a
supporting half-plane of it, and on this family the ratios the clauses
supply are never tighter than the ones the polytope already implies. The
obstruction is the rate at which the cone narrows, $\lambda=1-2^{1-j}$
per round, and adding further cones through the same apex does not
change a rate. What would be needed to defeat that as well is a two-player
wedge, and \Cref{thm:seed-dichotomy} says any such instance is a stopping game
on which switch-all improvement has not converged --- the same pivot that
\Cref{cor:f-auso} and \Cref{prop:auso-size} leave open.
\end{remark}


\section{Summary}\label{sec:summary}

\paragraph{Foundations, proved from first principles.} The monotone
fixed-point theory of the Shapley operator (\Cref{sec:operators}); for
stopping games, a strategy-independent absorption estimate with exponent
$|\Vavg|$ (\Cref{lem:absorption}), the resulting contraction and
uniqueness of fixed points (\Cref{thm:contraction}), and determinacy
together with the identification of those fixed points with the game
values (\Cref{thm:stopping-determinacy}) --- so that outside
\Cref{thm:determinacy} nothing classical is imported. A denominator bound
$8^{r/2}$ depending only on the number $r$ of average vertices
(\Cref{lem:denominator}), sharpened to $2^{r}$ and shown attained
(\Cref{lem:denominator-sharp}), and an explicit reduction to the stopping
case with every constant computed (\Cref{thm:stopping-transform}).
Without any stopping hypothesis, $\val$ and every $\val_\sigma$ are the
\emph{least} fixed points of their operators (\Cref{thm:lfp-general}),
proved by a first-passage decomposition rather than by exchanging a limit
with an infimum.

\paragraph{Positive algorithmic results.} Several tractable regimes, each
with a complete proof, and each whose algorithm we implemented and checked
against exhaustive computation:
arbitrary SSGs with $a=O(\log N)$ average vertices, in time
$\mathrm{poly}(N)2^{a}$ and with no stopping hypothesis
(\Cref{thm:few-avg}), the exponent being improvable to the escape
exponent $d(G)\le a$, which \Cref{prop:fk-family} separates from $a$ by
$\Theta(N)$ (\Cref{thm:few-escape}); games whose cycles are \emph{pure}, that is in which
no cycle passes through an average vertex (\Cref{thm:avg-acyclic}) or,
dually, none passes through a controlled vertex
(\Cref{thm:player-free}), both in polynomial time; one-player games, by
an explicit linear program (\Cref{thm:one-player}); all three being
instances of one theorem that is strictly stronger than the three
together, namely that it suffices for \emph{some single colour} to be
absent from every cycle (\Cref{thm:kacyclic,prop:kacyclic-strict});
games in which no cycle carries two Max vertices, and more generally in
which the components of the Max-reachability digraph have size
$O(\log N)$ (\Cref{thm:bounded-components}), a class that
\Cref{prop:k1-family} shows meets none of the others; a quantitative
class defined by a linear-programming certificate for the rate at which
two-sided value iteration contracts (\Cref{thm:escape-class}), with a
family inside it and outside all four combinatorial ones
(\Cref{prop:escape-family}); the stopping games whose value alphabet has
a polynomially bounded common denominator, solved in $O(N^{2}D^{2})$ with
no advance knowledge of $D$ (\Cref{thm:few-denominator}) --- a class read
off the answer and not the presentation, which \Cref{prop:fv-family}
shows lies inside none of the five; and, in general, a
Las~Vegas algorithm of expected time $e^{2\sqrt n}\,\mathrm{poly}(N)$
obtained from a full analysis of random-facet strategy improvement
(\Cref{thm:rf-correct,thm:rf-bound,lem:fbound,thm:subexp}). The last is
subexponential, not polynomial, and is the strongest general upper bound
here.

\paragraph{Where the difficulty is.} The optimal Max strategies of a
stopping game form exactly the subcube of greedy strategies
(\Cref{thm:opt-subcube}), and from every strategy at most $|\Vmax|$
single strictly improving switches reach it (\Cref{thm:short-path}). So
no lower bound can come from the length of improving paths
(\Cref{cor:selection}); everything is in the \emph{selection} of
switches. That selection problem is exactly the original problem:
\textsc{SSG-Value} is polynomial-time equivalent to comparing two vertex
values, $n$ non-adaptive comparisons determine an optimal strategy
(\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence}), and deciding whether one given vertex
is badly chosen is again equivalent to the whole problem
(\Cref{cor:wrong-equivalence}). The information content is smaller than the
first count suggests --- the order of the average values already determines
everything (\Cref{thm:order-determines}) --- but not the difficulty, since
each comparison is again an instance of the problem.

\paragraph{Refutations and barriers, each with an explicit instance.}
All-switches does not dominate partial switches
(\Cref{thm:all-switches-refuted}) and can take more than $|\Vmax|$
iterations while undoing its own choices
(\Cref{prop:allswitch-overshoot}), while the least-index and
smallest-gap rules take $2^{(N-2)/2}-1$ iterations on an acyclic
one-player ladder that back-substitution solves in linear time
(\Cref{thm:ladder}); the induced orientation of the
combined two-player cube can be cyclic, so no potential lives on it
(\Cref{thm:cyclic-uso}); value iteration needs $2^{\Omega(N)}$ steps
already on games with no players at all (\Cref{thm:vi-lower}); there is
no naive Hamming potential (\Cref{thm:hamming-refuted}) and seven natural
polynomial-time selection rules each fail (\Cref{prop:rules-fail}), while
the wrong vertices can be a $1/|\Vmax|$ fraction of the switchable ones
(\Cref{prop:needle}); the exact gain of a switch is a bounded transfer
impedance divided by an escape probability that can be $2^{-\Theta(N)}$,
so no potential of polynomial range can work (\Cref{thm:impedance});
values live on a grid of mesh $2^{-a}$, which caps \emph{every} improving
rule at $N2^{a}$ switches and is essentially attained by the ladder
(\Cref{thm:switch-count}), so counting arguments of that shape are
settled in both directions; and
no parameter blind to which player owns which vertex can serve as a
hardness certificate, since it is already large on easy one-player
instances (\Cref{rem:owner-blind}); and no sound rule deciding a single
vertex can read only a bounded-radius neighbourhood of it
(\Cref{prop:locality}). Finally, solving the deterministic part of the
game exactly --- an acceleration that collapses the ladder's exponential
run to a single round (\Cref{prop:residue-ladder}) --- is undone by a
value-preserving normalisation adding at most $4N$ average vertices,
which leaves no redex for a preprocessing step to recognise, so no rule
of that kind runs in polynomially many rounds unless all-switches already
does (\Cref{thm:normalform-barrier}); the level above it, which frees one
average vertex, escapes and is a sound improving rule that is not a
switching rule at all (\Cref{prop:freeze-escapes}), but only because the
padding was too short --- lengthening each of its gadgets to $k+1$ links
blinds every rule that frees at most $k$ average vertices, so the whole
hierarchy is covered for every polynomially bounded $k$
(\Cref{thm:window-barrier}). Finally, the one general
hammer for minimising a polynomial-time computable function over a strategy
cube is submodularity, and the objective here is not submodular under any
orientation of either cube, on a five-vertex witness
(\Cref{lem:readonce,prop:no-submodular}).

\paragraph{Mechanisms, and where they stop.} Three sound polynomial-time
comparison mechanisms that \emph{propagate} are developed and measured
against one yardstick, and the second of them turns out to be an endpoint
of a continuum.
The value-simulation preorder (\Cref{sec:simorder}) is arithmetic-free,
returns all $N^{2}$ verdicts from one greatest fixed point, and beats
every bounded radius; it fails on branch compensation, and
\Cref{thm:matching-barrier} shows every calculus of its class fails, by a
fooling pair. The slack calculus (\Cref{sec:slack}) repairs exactly that
and is cheap in precision, but on non-interacting parts it is two-sided
value iteration (\Cref{thm:slack-barrier}), hence exponential on an
explicit family --- for its own clauses only: adding transitivity, whose
premises leave the separated region, turns that family from
$2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds into $4m-3$ (\Cref{prop:trans-Hm}), and no
stall on a stopping game is permanent at all, since the calculus computes
$w^\ast$ exactly in the limit (\Cref{thm:trans-complete}). Permanent
blindness exists only where $T$ has a second fixed point, hence never on
a stopping game (\Cref{lem:phi-certificate,prop:trans-stall}), and that
is not an accident: a certificate of the form $\varphi(x)-\psi(y)$ that
survives a min-plus closure must have $\varphi=\psi$ and must be a
fixed point of $T$, so on a stopping game it is the exact limit and
proves nothing about the rate (\Cref{thm:separable}); what escapes has
vanishing diagonal (\Cref{cor:set-certificate}). Read multiplicatively
instead of additively, the same calculus becomes the ratio calculus of
\Cref{sec:ratio}, which is incomparable with the additive one pair by
pair, is not an instance of it --- its average clauses are the harmonic
mean and the mediant --- and yet obeys the same two-sided bound on the
same configurations and stalls on the same family for the same number of
rounds (\Cref{thm:ratio-sandwich,cor:ratio-stall}); the two are the ends
of a one-parameter family every member of which stalls identically,
because the quantity deciding whether the bound is informative does not
involve the parameter (\Cref{prop:mobius}). The classical nonlinear
Perron--Frobenius route that motivates the multiplicative reading is
closed separately: after homogenisation the cone spectral radius is
identically $1$, and the Collatz--Wielandt bracket can be $[1,2]$ while
the vector it brackets is off by $2^{N-3}$ (\Cref{prop:cw}). Seeding it from an evaluated bracket
(\Cref{sec:seeded}) strictly dominates both, and decides every stalling
instance in this document --- including a vertex that
\Cref{prop:bracket}(a) forbids any bracket from deciding --- while
\Cref{thm:seeded-barrier} shows the geometry, not the initialisation, was
always the obstruction. A fourth mechanism does not propagate at all: the
linear program of \Cref{sec:transport} optimises over the monotonicity
$Tw^\ast=w^\ast$ imposes, is exact on successor-closed average parts
(\Cref{lem:transport-exact}), and therefore settles in one solve both
$G_8$ and the family $H_m$ on which the slack calculus stalls
exponentially. On a seven-vertex game it fails in turn to order the two
successors, and \Cref{thm:transport-barrier} shows why that failure is
complementary: what defeats the pair test is a controlled vertex lying on a
cycle through the branch it must be compared against, and there the test needs
its seed to localise the value to within $2^{-\Theta(N)}$. It is a pair test
that fails, not the mechanism: \Cref{rem:own-successor} observes that at a Max
vertex the row $x(v)\ge x(v^{(i)})$ is already in $Q(G)$, so a negative
$\operatorname{Sep}(v,v^{(i)})$ decides $v$ outright, and it is negative on
every instance in question.

Since the two failures are complementary, the two mechanisms are coupled
in \Cref{sec:hybrid}, and the coupling is a feedback loop rather than a
disjunction: the halves of the controlled rows that a linear programme
must drop, being non-convex, are exactly the ones whose consequences for
differences the slack clauses supply, while the average equalities the
slack calculus can only reach by iterating are what the programme solves
in one step. The result is the sharpest mechanism here.
\Cref{thm:hybrid-complete} shows that on a stopping game every closed
matrix has $K(\Delta)=\{w^\ast\}$ --- the polytope collapses to the value
vector, not merely to a decided pair --- and that the argument touches the
programme only at the $4N$ pairs containing a sink
(\Cref{cor:hybrid-sink}), so the hybrid is a box-propagation loop whose
only fixed point is the exact one. It disposes of every stall recorded in
this document, in one round or two (\Cref{prop:hybrid-decides}). What is
left is a rate, and \Cref{prop:hybrid-rate} reduces even that to the decay
of one scalar sequence: the box has width $\omega_k$, a bound
$\omega_k\le C\rho^{k}$ with $\rho\le1-1/\mathrm{poly}(N)$ would put the
problem in $\mathsf P$, and $\rho\to1$ is the only way the mechanism can
fail, and we do not know whether it does. What we do know is that the
evidence suggesting it does not was worthless: every instance in this
document on which the hybrid could be measured has one controlled vertex,
and \Cref{prop:hybrid-onectrl} shows that class is decided at round two
for free. \Cref{thm:hybrid-convex-barrier} supplies the method for lower
bounds --- a shrinking chain of convex certificates, replacing the box
certificate of \Cref{thm:slack-barrier}, which \Cref{rem:hybrid-barrier-lift}
shows is provably too weak here --- and \Cref{thm:hybrid-lower} applies it
to a one-player family with two Max vertices wired crosswise, each keeping
the other's box open, on which $\Delta_k(v^{(0)},v^{(1)})$ stays
nonnegative for $2^{\Omega(N)}$ rounds. That is not a refutation:
\Cref{rem:own-successor} observes that a decision rule should compare a
controlled vertex with its \emph{own} successor rather than its two
successors with each other, and on that family the useful entry is
negative at round one. The same correction applies to
\Cref{prop:transport-stalls} and \Cref{thm:transport-barrier}, which order
successors and do not stall as decision rules.

\paragraph{The gap.} \Cref{def:missing} states it at full strength and
\Cref{thm:gap-equivalence} proves it equivalent to a polynomial-time
algorithm. The same is true of a formally weaker-looking demand: a rule
that resolves a single vertex per round, on a game it may simplify in
between, already suffices (\Cref{thm:decide-one-bit}). What is
\emph{not} established anywhere above is a polynomial-time mechanism for
producing even one of the $n$ comparison bits of
\Cref{thm:compare-equivalence} --- equivalently, for selecting a
switchable vertex at which the current strategy is wrong. And that bit is
irreducibly global: two instances agreeing on the radius-$k$
neighbourhood of a vertex, for any fixed $k$, can reverse the answer
there (\Cref{prop:locality}). That is the whole of the remaining gap, and
it is the whole of the problem. \Cref{thm:seed-dichotomy} locates it once
more: any instance defeating the seeded rule with polynomially many
improvement rounds is a stopping SSG on which switch-all improvement
itself runs superpolynomially long, which no construction here achieves.

\end{document}
